What to know about your Blue Button report
- If you print or download your Blue Button report, you'll need to take responsibility for protecting the information in the report.
- This report doesn't include information you entered yourself. To find information you entered yourself, go back to the previous version of Blue Button on the My HealtheVet website.

Need help?
- If you have questions about this report or you need to add information to your records, send a secure message to your care team.
- If you're ever in crisis and need to talk with someone right away, call the Veterans Crisis Line at 988. Then select 1.

HEMOGLOBIN A1C on August 28, 2025, 3:45 p.m.
-----------------------------------------------------
Details about this test:
 
Site or sample tested: BLOOD
Ordered by: DAVID H KAMP
Location: BATTLE-CREEK.MED.VA.GOV
Date completed: August 28, 2025, 3:45 p.m.
Provider notes: Values obtained from A1C measurements can vary.  For typical A1C
assays, a reported value of 7.0 could actually be between 6.72 and
7.28 if measured by a reference method.  A reported value of 9.0
could actually be between 8.73 and 9.27.  Ref:
http://www.ngsp.org/CAPdata.asp
Target A1C values should be individualized.  Better understanding
of A1C test result accuracy is essential if clinicians are to
interpret results for Veterans, and discuss treatment options
through the process of Shared Decision Making.  For questions
regarding the performance characteristics of this test, clinicians
should contact the laboratory.  Patients should contact their
clinician.

Reference range: <5.69 %

Provider notes: For Test: HEMOGLOBIN A1C
1010,Values obtained from A1C measurements can vary.  For typical A1C
assays, a reported value of 7.0 could actually be between 6.72 and
7.28 if measured by a reference method.  A reported value of 9.0
could actually be between 8.73 and 9.27.  Ref:
http://www.ngsp.org/CAPdata.asp
Target A1C values should be individualized.  Better understanding
of A1C test result accuracy is essential if clinicians are to
interpret results for Veterans, and discuss treatment options
through the process of Shared Decision Making.  For questions
regarding the performance characteristics of this test, clinicians
should contact the laboratory.  Patients should contact their
clinician.

patient without allowing clearance of fluorescein is not
recommended.

        Grade         Definition
       0            No tenderness
       I            Tenderness to palpation WITHOUT grimace or flinch
       II           Tenderness WITH grimace &/or flinch to palpation
       III          Tenderness with WITHDRAWAL (+ "Jump Sign")
       IV           Withdrawal (+ "Jump Sign") to non-noxious stimuli
                       (ie. superficial palpation, pin prick, gentle
                        percussion) 




   SCALE: (0= completely ankylosed, 1= very hypomobile, 2= slightly 
hypomobile, 3= normal, 4= slightly hypermobile, 5= very hypermobile, 6= 
complete 
instability)

measured

REHABILITATION VISIT [X] PT 

DATE OF VISIT: 05/28/2025

DURATION OF VISIT: 45 minutes 

TREATMENT: #6

TREATMENT DIAGNOSIS: pain in right shoulder

RE-EVAL DATE: 06/02/2025

PRECAUTIONS/LIMITATIONS: Left inguinal hernia, hyperlipidemia, post-surgical 
precautions (surg date 4/15/2025) (no heavy lifting with right upper 
extremity, no reaching into right shoulder extension with right elbow 
extended)

SUBJECTIVE: Veteran reports doing okay, been working on car with his son. 
Reports he has pushed himself up from the ground using Right UE, but then 
stopped doing it as it was slightly painful.  He experiences "pinching" on top, 
medial right shoulder when lifting arm out infront. Veteran is 6wks post op.

PAIN
Number: not rated
Location: top,medial right hsoulder 
Quality of pain: [x] better    [ ] worse   [ ] same

TREATMENT PLAN (eval: );
- Manual Therapy
 - Significant emphasis on but not limited to soft tissue massage 
(effleurage in clockwise or counterclockwise motions)/trigger point release 
(sustained holds) to right latissimus dorsi/right subscapularis
 

Protocol found at: 
https://www.massgeneral.org/assets/mgh/pdf/orthopaedics/sports-
medicine/physical-therapy/rehabilitation-protocol-for-biceps-tenodesis.pdf

- PHASE I: IMMEDIATE POST-OP: Passive Range of Motion Phase (1-4 WEEKS AFTER 
SURGERY) Rehabilitation Goals * Protect repaired biceps tendon. * Minimize 
shoulder pain and inflammatory response. * Keep incisions clean and dry. * 
Restore passive range of motion (PROM) of shoulder and elbow. * Adequate 
scapular function. Sling * Wear sling as directed by surgeon. * Wean out of 
sling starting 3 weeks post-op Precautions * No active range of motion (AROM) 
of the elbow or shoulder. * No shoulder external rotation beyond 40 degrees. 
* No shoulder extension or horizontal abduction past neutral. * Place a towel 
roll or pillow under elbow while laying supine to avoid shoulder extension * 
No lifting objects. * No friction massage to the proximal biceps/tenodesis 
site. Intervention Range of motion/Mobility * PROM of elbow for 
flexion/extension,supination/pronation. * AROM of wrist/hand * Shoulder PROM: 
avoid shoulder ER past 40 degrees and no shoulder extension beyond neutral 
Strengthening * Scapular retractions and mobility exercises * Ball squeezes 
Criteria to Progress * Appropriate healing of surgical incision. * Adequate 
pain control. * Full PROM of shoulder and elbow


- PHASE II: INTERMEDIATE POST-OP: ACTIVE RANGE OF MOTION PHASE (4-6 WEEKS 
AFTER 
SURGERY) Rehabilitation Goals * Minimize shoulder pain and inflammatory 
response. * Achieve gradual restoration of shoulder and elbow AROM. * Begin 
light waist-level functional activities. * Initial submaximal shoulder 
isometrics * Return to light computer or desk work. Precautions * No lifting 
with affected upper extremity. * No loading to biceps, elbow flexors, 
supinators. * No friction massage to the proximal biceps tendon/tenodesis 
site. * No running. * Avoid over stressing repaired tissue with stretching or 
manual therapy Additional Intervention *Continue with Phase I interventions 
Range of motion/Mobility * Shoulder AAROM o Lawn chair AAROM o Shoulder ER o 
Rail slides 
o Wall slides * Shoulder AROM o Supine shoulder flexion o Standing scaption o 
Shoulder ER in neutral o Shoulder ER @ 90 degrees supported on table * Elbow 
AROM o Active elbow flexion o Active elbow extension o Forearm supination o 
Forearm pronation Strengthening * Shoulder Isometrics o Flexion, extension, 
ER, IR, abduction Manual Therapy * Glenohumeral, scapulothoracic, and trunk 
joint mobilizations as indicated (Grade I-IV) * Posterior capsule stretching 
o Cross body stretching o Sleeper Stretch Cardiovascular exercise * Walking 
or 
stationary bike - avoid excessive weight bearing through affected arm. No 
distractive forces on shoulder Criteria to Progress * Full AROM of shoulder 
and 
elbow. * Proper scapular mechanics with ROM and functional activities. * 
Adequate pain control
- PHASE III: LATE POST-OP: INITIAL STRENGTHENING PHASE (6-8 WEEKS AFTER 
SURGERY) 
Rehabilitation Goals * Normalize strength, endurance, neuromuscular control. 
* 
Return to chest-level activities. Precautions * No strengthening or 
functional 
activities until near full ROM is achieved. * Avoid long-lever arm resistance 
for elbow flexion and supination. Additional Intervention Strengthening * 
Continue shoulder and elbow PROM and AROM * Initiate Resisted Biceps curls 
*Continue with Phase I-II Interventions * Initiate Resisted supination * 
Resisted Triceps extension * Resisted wrist extension/Resisted wrist flexion 
* 
Continue shoulder isometrics o Progress resistance as tolerated * Rhythmic 
stabilizations Criteria to Progress * Full shoulder and elbow AROM. * Good 
tolerance to initial strengthening without increase in symptoms.
- PHASE IV: Advanced Strengthening (8-12 WEEKS AFTER SURGERY) Rehabilitation 
Goals * Maintain full pain-free shoulder and elbow AROM. * Progress shoulder 
and 
elbow strength. Focus on low load, high repetitions (30-50). Open and closed 
chain strengthening. Additional Intervention *Continue with Phase I-III 
interventions Strengthening * Resisted IR in neutral * Resisted ER in neutral 
* 
Resisted shoulder IR in elevation * Resisted shoulder ER in elevation * Full 
can 
scapular plane arm elevation * Side-lying ER * Prone Rowing o 30/45/90 
degrees 
abduction * Push up plus progression (wall, counter, knees on floor, floor) * 
Resisted PNF Diagonals Cardiovascular exercise * Can initiate return to 
running 
* No swimming Criteria to Progress * 5/5 shoulder and elbow strength. * Full 
shoulder AROM in all planes. * Good tolerance to strengthening exercise 
without 
increase in symptoms
- PHASE V: RETURN-TO-SPORT (12-16 WEEKS AFTER SURGERY) Rehabilitation Goals * 
Progress strength and function of involved upper extremity. * Return to 
normal 
sport or work activities. * Maintain pain-free ROM. * Avoid excessive 
anterior 
capsule stress. Additional Intervention *Continue with Phase II-IV 
interventions 
Strengthening/Sport Specific training * Initiate plyometric training starting 
with below shoulder level and progressing to overhead: Weighted ball 
drop/catch
in standing, chest pass, overhead ball dribble against wall, prone 90/90 ball 
drop/catch, prone Y ball drop/catch, prone T ball drop/catch * Multi 
joint/compound strengthening * Interval return to sport specific training 
Criteria to Progress * No pain with progressive strengthening. * 90% strength 
of 
involved extremity compared to uninvolved side with dynamometry testing * 
Within 
normal limits with field testing if applicable (e.g. closed kinetic chain 
upper 
extremity stability test, single arm seated shot-put test, ASH test/Modified 
ASH 
test). * Low level to no disability with patient reported outcome measure 
(e.g. 
Quick DASH)

AAROM in a painfree range of motion


TREATMENT PROVIDED:
[2] Therapeutic Exercises (30mins)
- Pulley's in flexion and scaption, wall slides with towel  2x10 each
- standing right shoulder submaximal isometrics 1x10, 3 second holds in each 
direction (flexion, abduction, external rotation, internal rotation, 
extension)
-standing right shoulder abduction AAROM wall slides 2x10
- Sidelying scapular retraction, sliding right hand on table x10reps
- Seated Scapular Retraction  2x10reps

[1] Manual Therapy (15mins)
Veteran in left sidelying ? 
-PROM right shoulder flexion/scaption/abduction/external 
rotation to less than 40 degrees, 2x5 in each direction
-gentle STM to right pectoralis, mid trap/rhomboids, Upper Trap and trigger 
point release of right latissimus dorsi and right subscapularis into right 
shoulder flexion

mobilizations as indicated (Grade I-IV) * Posterior capsule stretching o Cross 
body stretching o Sleeper Stretch Cardiovascular exercise * Walking or 
stationary bike - avoid excessive weight bearing through affected arm. No 
distractive forces on shoulder Criteria to Progress * Full AROM of shoulder and 
elbow. * Proper scapular mechanics with ROM and functional activities. * 
Adequate pain control
- PHASE III: LATE POST-OP: INITIAL STRENGTHENING PHASE (6-8 WEEKS AFTER SURGERY) 
Rehabilitation Goals * Normalize strength, endurance, neuromuscular control. * 
Return to chest-level activities. Precautions * No strengthening or functional 
activities until near full ROM is achieved. * Avoid long-lever arm resistance 
for elbow flexion and supination. Additional Intervention Strengthening * 
Continue shoulder and elbow PROM and AROM * Initiate Resisted Biceps curls 
*Continue with Phase I-II Interventions * Initiate Resisted supination * 
Resisted Triceps extension * Resisted wrist extension/Resisted wrist flexion * 
Continue shoulder isometrics o Progress resistance as tolerated * Rhythmic 
stabilizations Criteria to Progress * Full shoulder and elbow AROM. * Good 
tolerance to initial strengthening without increase in symptoms.
- PHASE IV: Advanced Strengthening (8-12 WEEKS AFTER SURGERY) Rehabilitation 
Goals * Maintain full pain-free shoulder and elbow AROM. * Progress shoulder and 
elbow strength. Focus on low load, high repetitions (30-50). Open and closed 
chain strengthening. Additional Intervention *Continue with Phase I-III 
interventions Strengthening * Resisted IR in neutral * Resisted ER in neutral * 
Resisted shoulder IR in elevation * Resisted shoulder ER in elevation * Full can 
scapular plane arm elevation * Side-lying ER * Prone Rowing o 30/45/90 degrees 
abduction * Push up plus progression (wall, counter, knees on floor, floor) * 
Resisted PNF Diagonals Cardiovascular exercise * Can initiate return to running 
* No swimming Criteria to Progress * 5/5 shoulder and elbow strength. * Full 
shoulder AROM in all planes. * Good tolerance to strengthening exercise without 
increase in symptoms
- PHASE V: RETURN-TO-SPORT (12-16 WEEKS AFTER SURGERY) Rehabilitation Goals * 
Progress strength and function of involved upper extremity. * Return to normal 
sport or work activities. * Maintain pain-free ROM. * Avoid excessive anterior 
capsule stress. Additional Intervention *Continue with Phase II-IV interventions 
Strengthening/Sport Specific training * Initiate plyometric training starting 
with below shoulder level and progressing to overhead: Weighted ball drop/catch
in standing, chest pass, overhead ball dribble against wall, prone 90/90 ball 
drop/catch, prone Y ball drop/catch, prone T ball drop/catch * Multi 
joint/compound strengthening * Interval return to sport specific training 
Criteria to Progress * No pain with progressive strengthening. * 90% strength of 
involved extremity compared to uninvolved side with dynamometry testing * Within 
normal limits with field testing if applicable (e.g. closed kinetic chain upper 
extremity stability test, single arm seated shot-put test, ASH test/Modified ASH 
test). * Low level to no disability with patient reported outcome measure (e.g. 
Quick DASH)

AAROM in a painfree range of motion

                       (ie. superficial palpation, pin prick, gentle
                        percussion)

ASSESSMENT

The Veteran is a 48-year-old male Veteran who presents to physical therapy with 
a primary complaint of right shoulder pain 4 weeks (surgery on 4/15/25) post 
operative right biceps brachii tenodesis repair and shoulder debridement follow 
right SLAP tear. The Veteran demonstrated objective deficits that included: 
decreased right upper strength, decreased right upper extremity AROM/PROM, pain, 
grade 1 tenderness to palpation in right biceps brachii muscle belly/tenodesis 
attachment to right humerus and moderate tissue tension in right latissimus 
dorsi/right subscapularis. These deficits affect the Veteran's ability to reach 
and lift heavy/light objects with right upper extremity. The Veteran would 
benefit from skilled physical therapy to address these deficits and to improve 
overall function. 


Moderate Complexity Evaluation
  - History: 1-2 personal factors/comorbidities
  - Exam: 3+ body systems involved
  - Presentation: evolving/changing


PLAN 
Specific Tx Modalities, methods, techniques: 

- Manual Therapy
      - Significant emphasis on but not limited to soft tissue massage 
(effleurage in clockwise or counterclockwise motions)/trigger point release 
(sustained holds) to right latissimus dorsi/right subscapularis
 

Protocol found at: 
https://www.massgeneral.org/assets/mgh/pdf/orthopaedics/sports-
medicine/physical-therapy/rehabilitation-protocol-for-biceps-tenodesis.pdf

- PHASE I: IMMEDIATE POST-OP: Passive Range of Motion Phase (1-4 WEEKS AFTER 
SURGERY) Rehabilitation Goals * Protect repaired biceps tendon. * Minimize 
shoulder pain and inflammatory response. * Keep incisions clean and dry. * 
Restore passive range of motion (PROM) of shoulder and elbow. * Adequate 
scapular function. Sling * Wear sling as directed by surgeon. * Wean out of 
sling starting 3 weeks post-op Precautions * No active range of motion (AROM) of 
the elbow or shoulder. * No shoulder external rotation beyond 40 degrees. * No 
shoulder extension or horizontal abduction past neutral. * Place a towel roll or 
pillow under elbow while laying supine to avoid shoulder extension * No lifting 
objects. * No friction massage to the proximal biceps/tenodesis site. 
Intervention Range of motion/Mobility * PROM of elbow for flexion/extension, 
supination/pronation. * AROM of wrist/hand * Shoulder PROM: avoid shoulder ER 
past 40 degrees and no shoulder extension beyond neutral Strengthening * 
Scapular retractions and mobility exercises * Ball squeezes Criteria to Progress 
* Appropriate healing of surgical incision. * Adequate pain control. * Full PROM 
of shoulder and elbow
- PHASE II: INTERMEDIATE POST-OP: ACTIVE RANGE OF MOTION PHASE (4-6 WEEKS AFTER 
SURGERY) Rehabilitation Goals * Minimize shoulder pain and inflammatory 
response. * Achieve gradual restoration of shoulder and elbow AROM. * Begin 
light waist-level functional activities. * Initial submaximal shoulder 
isometrics * Return to light computer or desk work. Precautions * No lifting 
with affected upper extremity. * No loading to biceps, elbow flexors, 
supinators. * No friction massage to the proximal biceps tendon/tenodesis site. 
* No running. * Avoid over stressing repaired tissue with stretching or manual 
therapy Additional Intervention *Continue with Phase I interventions Range of 
motion/Mobility * Shoulder AAROM o Lawn chair AAROM o Shoulder ER o Rail slides 
o Wall slides * Shoulder AROM o Supine shoulder flexion o Standing scaption o 
Shoulder ER in neutral o Shoulder ER @ 90 degrees supported on table * Elbow 
AROM o Active elbow flexion o Active elbow extension o Forearm supination o 
Forearm pronation Strengthening * Shoulder Isometrics o Flexion, extension, ER, 
IR, abduction Manual Therapy * Glenohumeral, scapulothoracic, and trunk joint 
mobilizations as indicated (Grade I-IV) * Posterior capsule stretching o Cross 
body stretching o Sleeper Stretch Cardiovascular exercise * Walking or 
stationary bike - avoid excessive weight bearing through affected arm. No 
distractive forces on shoulder Criteria to Progress * Full AROM of shoulder and 
elbow. * Proper scapular mechanics with ROM and functional activities. * 
Adequate pain control
- PHASE III: LATE POST-OP: INITIAL STRENGTHENING PHASE (6-8 WEEKS AFTER SURGERY) 
Rehabilitation Goals * Normalize strength, endurance, neuromuscular control. * 
Return to chest-level activities. Precautions * No strengthening or functional 
activities until near full ROM is achieved. * Avoid long-lever arm resistance 
for elbow flexion and supination. Additional Intervention Strengthening * 
Continue shoulder and elbow PROM and AROM * Initiate Resisted Biceps curls 
*Continue with Phase I-II Interventions * Initiate Resisted supination * 
Resisted Triceps extension * Resisted wrist extension/Resisted wrist flexion * 
Continue shoulder isometrics o Progress resistance as tolerated * Rhythmic 
stabilizations Criteria to Progress * Full shoulder and elbow AROM. * Good 
tolerance to initial strengthening without increase in symptoms.
- PHASE IV: Advanced Strengthening (8-12 WEEKS AFTER SURGERY) Rehabilitation 
Goals * Maintain full pain-free shoulder and elbow AROM. * Progress shoulder and 
elbow strength. Focus on low load, high repetitions (30-50). Open and closed 
chain strengthening. Additional Intervention *Continue with Phase I-III 
interventions Strengthening * Resisted IR in neutral * Resisted ER in neutral * 
Resisted shoulder IR in elevation * Resisted shoulder ER in elevation * Full can 
scapular plane arm elevation * Side-lying ER * Prone Rowing o 30/45/90 degrees 
abduction * Push up plus progression (wall, counter, knees on floor, floor) * 
Resisted PNF Diagonals Cardiovascular exercise * Can initiate return to running 
* No swimming Criteria to Progress * 5/5 shoulder and elbow strength. * Full 
shoulder AROM in all planes. * Good tolerance to strengthening exercise without 
increase in symptoms
- PHASE V: RETURN-TO-SPORT (12-16 WEEKS AFTER SURGERY) Rehabilitation Goals * 
Progress strength and function of involved upper extremity. * Return to normal 
sport or work activities. * Maintain pain-free ROM. * Avoid excessive anterior 
capsule stress. Additional Intervention *Continue with Phase II-IV interventions 
Strengthening/Sport Specific training * Initiate plyometric training starting 
with below shoulder level and progressing to overhead: Weighted ball drop/catch 
in standing, chest pass, overhead ball dribble against wall, prone 90/90 ball 
drop/catch, prone Y ball drop/catch, prone T ball drop/catch * Multi 
joint/compound strengthening * Interval return to sport specific training 
Criteria to Progress * No pain with progressive strengthening. * 90% strength of 
involved extremity compared to uninvolved side with dynamometry testing * Within 
normal limits with field testing if applicable (e.g. closed kinetic chain upper 
extremity stability test, single arm seated shot-put test, ASH test/Modified ASH 
test). * Low level to no disability with patient reported outcome measure (e.g. 
Quick DASH)

TREATMENT GOALS 
 
Short-Term Goals: (2 weeks) 
1.      The Veteran will self-report performance of home exercise program 3-7 
times per week in order to be independent/compliant with home exercise program.
 
 Long-term goals: (4 weeks) 
2.      The Veteran will indicate at least a 50% decrease in right shoulder pain 
in order to tolerate reaching above shoulder height with right upper extremity. 
3.      The Veteran will demonstrate improved strength in right shoulder flexors 
to at least 3/5 in order to tolerate reaching above shoulder height with right 
upper extremity. 
4.      The Veteran will demonstrate improved right shoulder flexion active 
range of motion to at least 60 degrees in order to tolerate reaching above 
shoulder height with right upper extremity. 
5.      The Veteran will demonstrate improved PROMIS score by at least 5 points 
in order to improve overall function. 
 
 
 
 
 
Discharge Plan: 
[ ] Continue until goals met 
[ ] Reassessment in two weeks 
[x ] Reassessment in 4 weeks, (2x a week for 4 weeks) 
[ ] Discharge from PT services

Physical Therapist

options discussed. After thorough discussion, patient elected to proceed with 
a right shoulder arthroscopy with long head biceps tenodesis, glenohumeral

glenohumeral joint space AC arthritis and no fracture dislocation or acute 
osseous abnormality.

Advanced Imaging: MRI shows some mild AC arthritis, complex SLAP tear which 
may Involve portions of the anterior posterior labrum with some frayed glenold 
cartilage hooking of the anterior acromion and bursitis.

Risks discussed: Pain, bleeding, infection, damage to surrounding 
tendons/nerves and vessels, implant failure, dislocation, failure to heal or 
non-union, continued pain, deep vein thrombosis leading to pulmonary embolus, 
stiffness, loss of function, revision surgery, and amputation. Anesthesia 
risks include heart attack, stroke, death, blindness. Patient understands this 
is not an all inclusive list of potential complications.

Dear Veteran,



I am sending you the results of your telederm consultation which recommended 
cyrotherapy for the actinic keratosis located on the bridge of your nose. 
Please call the PACT team to request an appointment for cryotherapy if you 
would like to pursue this treatment. 

The other lesions do not require treatment. 


EXAM: 
A: On the nasal bridge is a gritty erythematous papule with strawberry 
vessels on dermoscopy
B: Normal-appearing skin; unable to identify lesion of concern
C: Along the frontal hairline is a pink papule with regular vessels

OVERALL CONSULT/IMAGE QUALITY:
  Satisfactory with suggestions for improvement
    in  Focus

IMPRESSION BASED ON IMAGES AND INFORMATION REVIEWED:
  PROBLEM A:
    Diagnosis: actinic keratosis
  PROBLEM B:
    Diagnosis: normal skin, no concern for malignancy 
  PROBLEM C:
    Diagnosis: nevus 

RECOMMENDATIONS FOR REFERRING PROVIDER:
  Problem A:
    Other recommendations:
      You should treat with cryotherapy with liquid nitrogen, using at least
      two freeze-thaw cycles of 5 to 7 seconds each. Discuss risks of
      cryotherapy which include: permanent scar, loss or darkening of skin
      color, blister, and recurrence of lesion. If the lesion does not 
resolve
      after 2 to 3 months, re-treat with another round of cryotherapy. Only
      after the third failed treatment should patient be sent to dermatology.
      ("Three strikes and you are out" to the dermatologist.)
  Problem B:
    Other recommendations: No further treatment needed. If this consult does 
not adequately address the lesion of concern, consider a repeat telederm 
consult. Try to avoid pressing down firmly when photographing as this can 
distort the architecture of a lesion and obscure visualization. 
  Problem C:
    Other recommendations: No further treatment needed.


If you have any questions please call your PACT nurse at 1-616-249-5300.

Sincerely, 


David H. Kamp, DNP, AGNP-C

joint..

labrum at and posterior to the bicipital anchor, for example seen on coronal 
image 14 most consistent with a SLAP configuration type tear which appears to 
partially propagate into the posterior labrum. There is articular cartilage 
thinning at the glenohumeral joint without full-thickness chondral loss.
 
No suspicious marrow signal abnormality.
 
IMPRESSION:
 
1. There is signal alteration within the superior labrum at and posterior to the 
bicipital anchor most compatible with a SLAP configuration type tear.

Dear Veteran,



I have reviewed your recent radiology tests and posted the results below. Please 
follow up with neurosurgery should you have any questions. 


DOS:8/24/2024
Specialty: MRI-SPINE
 
FINDINGS: Multiecho multiplanar imaging is obtained.  There is straightening 
of the normal lordotic curve of the cervical spine which can be seen as a 
anatomic variant or the presence of muscle spasm.  Otherwise vertebral body 
alignment is maintained. Vertebral body heights are maintained.  With 
exception of the C7-T1 interspace remaining cervical interspaces demonstrate 
varying degrees of decreased signal on T2 weighting compatible with 
degenerative dehydration.  There is mild narrowing of the C3-4 through C5-6 
interspaces.  No acute marrow signal changes are demonstrated. 
Cervicomedullary junction and cervical spinal cord appear unremarkable.  No 
paraspinous abnormalities are demonstrated.
 
At the C2-3 level there are no significant findings.
 
At the C3-4 level there are bilateral hypertrophic uncovertebral changes 
causing moderately advanced bilateral foraminal narrowing, more severe on the 
left.  There is some mild narrowing of the ventral subarachnoid space due to 
endplate marginal spurring and mild annular bulge.
 
At the C4-5 level mild annular bulge with some moderate narrowing of the 
ventral subarachnoid space. Right-sided uncovertebral hypertrophy and facet 
hypertrophy causing moderate right foraminal narrowing, mild left foraminal 
narrowing due to the same processes.
 
At the C5-6 level there is moderate spinal canal stenosis, a small sliver of 
the dorsal subarachnoid space remains. There may be some mild cord flattening 
in AP dimension. There is advanced bilateral foraminal narrowing due to 
hypertrophic uncovertebral disease.  This is worse on the right.
 
At the C6-7 level there is moderately advanced spinal canal stenosis. There 
is a large broad-based right-sided disc extrusion which extends laterally 
causing severe right foraminal stenosis. Left-sided hypertrophic 
uncovertebral changes with moderate left foraminal narrowing.
 
C7-T1 level no significant findings.
 
IMPRESSION:
1.  Spinal canal stenosis at the C5-6 and C6-7 levels as discussed. At the 
C6-7 level there is a large broad-based right-sided disc extrusion with 
severe right foraminal stenosis.

2.  Multilevel uncovertebral hypertrophy resulting in multilevel bilateral 
foraminal narrowing as detailed above.

If you have any questions please call your PACT nurse at 1-616-249-5300.



Sincerely, 


David H. Kamp, DNP, AGNP-C

aggravated recently while cleaning out a pool. The vet reports his jerking

ASSESSMENT/PLAN:

Test results: A bilateral upper extremity EMG/NCS from 6/19/24 by Dr. 
McCausland was reviewed. This demonstrated right mild to moderate carpal 
tunnel syndrome without evidence of axon loss, and left mild severity carpal 
tunnel syndrome without evidence of axon loss. Assessment: Bilateral carpal 
tunnel syndrome, I reviewed the diagnosis with the patient. Treatment options 
for carpal tunnel syndrome were discussed with the patient, including non-
operative versus operative management. Non-operative treatment options include 
anti-inflammatory medication, nighttime wrist bracing, and occasionally 
corticosteroid injections. Alternatively, the condition can be treated 
surgically with carpal tunnel release. Given the severity and chronicity of 
his symptoms, I offered surgery. The risks of bilateral endoscopic versus open 
carpal tunnel release surgery were discussed, including but not limited to the 
risks of bleeding, infection, damaqe to surroundinq structures, stiffness, 
qeneral medical complications. 

related to anesthesia or surgery (such as blood clot, pulmonary embolus, 
stroke, or cardiac arrest), and persistent or recurrent symptoms and pain.
In more severe cases of carpal tunnel syndrome, it can take months for 
symptoms to improve after surgery, and it is possible for symptoms to be 
irreversible even despite surgery. I reviewed the post-operative 
rehabilitation. The patient understood and wished to proceed with surgery. He 
would like to start with the left side followed by the right at least 3 weeks 
later. Surgery will be scheduled at our earliest mutual convenience.

Dear Veteran:


This is in response to your request for Community Care services.


* You have been authorized for evaluation and treatment for a MRI. The
referral is valid for 60 DAYS from first scheduled appointment which is
subject to change based on the first appointment date.  Appointment needs
to be scheduled on or after 8/29/2023.   If you do not schedule an
appointment within 14 calendar days from the date of this letter, the
authorization for these services will be cancelled and returned to your VA
provider.  Any testing completed prior to your scheduled appointment will
not be covered unless you contact community care with the date and time of
that testing and your authorization date is updated.
You will need to call your Community Provider and Self Schedule an
appointment within 14 days.


A copy of the enclosed authorization has been faxed to:


Provider Name: TRINITY HEALTH PARTNERS
Provider Address:1500 E SHERMAN BLVD MUSKEGON, MI 49444
Provider Phone:231-672-4800
Authorization Number:  VA0031436925


If a representative of their office has not yet contacted you to schedule
your approved care, please call 269-223-5011 . You may also contact the
facility directly at 231-672-4800 for scheduling.


If you need to cancel or reschedule this appointment, please call your
non-VA provider office. Please follow up with the Battle Creek Medical
Center Community Care Office to provide us your first appointment date and
time.  If more convenient, you can send the appointment date via secure
messaging.  Requests for additional services must be submitted to the VA
Community Care Office at the Battle Creek Medical Center.


*Note to Community Care provider office:  If there is a need for further
intervention, testing, therapy, or visits, please fax
recommendations/treatment plan to (269)-223-6112. 


Emergency Services
During a medical or mental health emergency, the Department of Veterans
Affairs (VA) encourages Veterans to seek immediate medical attention
without delay.  Veterans do not need to check with VA before calling for
an ambulance or going to an emergency department.  In most instances
Veterans are eligible to receive authorized emergency care at an
in-network facility, if VA is notified of the emergent event within 72
hours of the start of care.


It is important that your visit to a community emergency room be reported
to VA as soon as possible because it allows VA to assist in coordinating
your care of transfer, and it helps ensure that administrative and
clinical requirements for VA to pay for the care are met.


Inform your Emergency Provider to Report Your Emergency Treatment to VA


"	Providers should report Your emergency treatment through VAs Emergency
Care Reporting (ECR) portal,
https://EmergencyCareReporting.CommunityCare.va.gov, or by calling;
844.72HRVHA (844.724.7842)	
"	They also need to contact the local VA medical center (VAMC) to
coordinate follow-up care and transfer activities, if necessary.
"	Phone numbers and email addresses to local VAMCs are available at.  
https://www.va.gov/find-locations/.


Veterans or their representatives can also report an episode of emergency
care to VA.  However, VA should only be notified once and ideally, that
notification is from Community Care facility staff where the Veteran is
being treated.  


Prescriptions
Your community provider may write a prescription related to the authorized
care. If there is an immediate need for your prescription from your
community care visit, you may be able to get your prescription, up to
14-day supply filled at a participating Community Care Network (CCN)
pharmacy.  To find a participating CCN pharmacy, please visit:
https://www.va.gov/find-locations.    If the participating CCN pharmacy
requests information regarding billing, information can be found at
https://www.va.gov/COMMUNITYCARE/docs/programs/OCC-Billing-Information-Ca
d.pdf
 


If you go to a non-CCN participating pharmacy, you will be responsible for
the cost of the medication and will not be able to seek reimbursement from
VA.   If you require more than a 14-day supply or if the prescribed
medication is not needed immediately, your community provider will send a
prescription to the VA pharmacy so that VA can provide you with your
routine medication.  Your community provider must follow the following
instructions:  
"	Prescriptions may be submitted via fax, in-person, or via
e-prescription. 
Fax# BC:  269.223.5519   Fax# Wyoming:  616.249.5484 
"	The prescription should include your full printed name and the
providers office address, office phone number, personal DEA registration
number (if prescribing a controlled substance), and personal NPI number.

The prescribed medications must be related to the services authorized on
the referral and must be included in the VA National Formulary.   Please
contact the VA pharmacy at your referring facility with any questions
regarding prescriptions, the VA Formulary, or non-formulary requests. 
An approved list of VA medications can be found on the VA National
Formulary at:  www.pbm.va.gov/NationalFormulary.asp, or by using the VA
National Formulary Search Tool at: 
www.pbm.va.gov/apps/VANationalFormulary
 Medical Devices
Your community provider may recommend that medical devices, adaptive
equipment, or other items be provided for the treatment or rehabilitation
of your medical condition.  Veterans are generally required to obtain
these items through the Prosthetics and Sensory Aids Service (PSAS) in
your referring facility.  For routine items your provider will complete a
VA form 10-10172, Community Care Provider-Request for Services form and
will fax it to the BCVA Community Care office. The Community Care office
will review this form, and the items will be sent to your home, or your
Community Providers office depending on their preferences
  
Please contact the Community Care Coordinator at the referring VA facility
indicated in the approved referral to ensure the prescription has been
received and that no additional information is required by VA.  VA PSAS
will determine whether the prescribed item or service is one that VA is
authorized to purchase. If not, you and the prescribing provider will work
to identify an alternative that will serve your clinical needs.  Your
community provider may provide medical devices or other items at the time
of healthcare service delivery or soon thereafter if there is an Urgent or
Emergent need.  



Co-Payments 
If you are required to pay a VA co-payment, you will be billed by the VA
for each authorized visit that you attend. However, you are NOT REQUIRED
to make co-payments to a Community Provider.
 
Additional Resources 
You may view your appointments online at My HealtheVet:
www.myhealth.va.gov.  You may locate Community Providers at VA Locator:
https://www.va.gov/find-locations/.



Thank you for the opportunity to serve you and for your service to our
great Nation!
 


Office of Community Care
5500 Armstrong Road
Battle Creek, Michigan 49037

9) Military Service

Title: DOD Military Service Information

NOTES:
1) This report may not show your complete DoD Military Service Information.
   Data prior to establishment of DEERS and full service reporting (c. 1980) 
   may not appear.
2) It is normal for the begin/end dates in DoD records, adjusted by the
   Personnel Center after separation, to vary slightly from the DD-214.
3) No peacetime deployments will be displayed.  For Gulf War I, only one
   period will be displayed even if you deployed more than once.  No conflict
   prior to Gulf War I will be displayed.  Kosovo, Bosnia, and Southern Watch
   data is incomplete and may not display.
4) For Guard/Reserve, periods of active duty may not display.  No periods of
   Active duty service less than 30 days will display.
